DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010468 | Process Optimizations for GDDR SGRAM in Automotive Applications : Topic: DI: Defect Inspection and Reduction
An investigation into latent failures due to the formation of a resistive interface at the metal-to-via connection in automotive grade graphics SGRAM. Process improvements in via formation, increased electrical coverage targeting signal degradation, and wafer level analysis to prevent test escapes during production have been proposed. DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010785 | High aspect ratio container edge profile tilting solution on advanced DRAM technology
We introduce a comprehensive solution to the High Aspect Ratio Container (HARC) dry etch process for the 1-beta DRAM process, achieving a sustainable, high-efficiency, and production-friendly solution. This method results in a 37% gain in Mean Time Between Clean (MTBC) and a 2.3% increase in etch chamber availability, leading to a 0.3% yield improvement. The solution includes a control method using adjustable edge RF (Radio Frequency) forward power to manage plasma drift in the chamber, and compensate edge ring height change to prevent plasma sheath distortion caused by edge ring erosion. This control method can be implemented in more advanced HARC plasma etch processes (1-gamma process) to enhance the performance.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010345 | Particle Control for Low-Energy Boron Implantation : CFM: Contamination-Free Manufacturing
We present a case study describing how particles added to wafers during dedicated low-energy boron (LEB) implants can be reduced by the implementation of new hardware to reduce the buildup of boron films on beamline components. The new hardware increases the ion beam angle of incidence on beamline component surfaces to enhance self-sputtering of films and significantly reduces the film growth rate on surfaces near the wafer being implanted. This is important because film growth can lead to delamination which is a particle source. Particle adders can reduce device yields.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010274 | Investigation of the Radially Spreading Micro-Bubble Defect due to Contamination from SEM
An investigation into the origin and control strategies for a distinct defect characterized by radially spreading wafer maps was conducted during the spin-on hard-mask (SOH), photo resist (PR), and spin-on dielectric (SOD) coating processes. It was found that this defect is closely associated with wafer review or critical dimension (CD) measurements using a scanning electron microscope (SEM) prior to the coating process, and exists in the form of micro-bubbles. To determine the formation mechanism, surface contamination analysis, contact angle, and potential measurements were performed. The results indicated a mechanism for bubble formation resulting from locally inhomogeneous wafer surface hydrophobization caused by carbon compound-based contamination in a vacuum SEM chamber, leading to bubble nucleation and air entrapment during the acceleration coating stage. As preventive measures, the organic contamination source was reduced by cleaning the SEM chamber, and it was demonstrated that implementing enhanced reducing resist consumption (RRC) in the photo process and treatments such as pre-wet cleaning or O2 plasma ashing are effective solutions.

Results show that these optimizations significantly reduced the void count and allowed for a reduced overall resistance of the metal layer, contributing to an increase in wafer yield.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010549 | Full Wafer Inspection for Voltage Contrast Systematic Defects Using High-Throughput Point Scan
A next generation system and methodology for high-throughput e-beam hot spot inspection is described. Rather than capturing images of each hot spot, just a single pixel centered on the signal node of each hot spot is collected and used to assess if the hot spot is defective or not. This innovation results in a very substantial savings in time per hot spot, and therefore a tremendous increase in throughput over previous generation hot spot inspection systems. Additional throughput improvement is gained by moving the stage continuously under the wafer, commonly referred to as continuous scan, as these individual pixels (aka points) are collected. Throughputs of 1 billion hot spots per hour are routinely achieved. The process for selecting hot spots for optimal scan speed and learning is explained. Back-end-of-line (BEOL) defects are binned based on unique path, allowing the source of primary defectivity to be statistically determined. Application of this system to a metal 2 missing-metal-fill defect issue affecting a recent Intel technology is described. By leveraging this system and split experiments involving progressively more stressed wafers, a 6 orders-of-magnitude drop in defectivity was accomplished relatively quickly preparing this technology for high-volume-manufacturing.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC64512.2025.11010569 | Wafer-Less Warm-up and Ramp-up Time Optimization for CMP Cost and Wastage Reduction : MS: Manufacturing for Sustainability
The use of warm-up wafers in Chemical Mechanical Planarization (CMP) processes represents a significant source of operational costs and inefficiencies in semiconductor manufacturing. Conventionally, 4 to 8 dummy wafers are utilized during the warm-up phase to condition polishing pads and stabilize slurry systems, ensuring optimal tool performance after maintenance or technology node transitions. However, as semiconductor technologies advance, featuring increasingly complex multi-layer architectures and intricate CMP steps, the reliance on dummy wafers has led to escalating operational costs, heightened resource consumption, and environmental concerns [1]. In response, this paper presents a novel Wafer-less Warm-up (WLWU) method that eliminates the need for dummy wafers by leveraging advanced pad conditioning techniques. WLWU simulates the effects of traditional warm-up processes by refreshing the polishing pad surface and optimizing slurry flow rates, ensuring production-ready conditions without compromising pad lifespan or process stability. Rigorous testing across various CMP process steps and toolsets demonstrates that WLWU can be effectively implemented without introducing inline variation or negatively impacting production yield. The findings underscore WLWU as a cost-effective and environmentally sustainable alternative to traditional warm-up procedures. By reducing dummy wafer usage, WLWU offers the semiconductor industry significant operational savings, potentially amounting to hundreds of millions of dollars annually. Additionally, it aligns with broader sustainability initiatives by minimizing material waste and resource consumption. This study highlights WLWU’s transformative potential, positioning it as a viable solution for enhancing efficiency and sustainability in CMP wafer fabrication.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551229 | AI-Driven Die-level Defect Disposition
This paper describes an AI-driven wafer disposition method that uses Mask R-CNN instance segmentation with automated guard-banding to handle complex, irregular defect patterns that are poorly screened by wafer-averaged limits and often require subjective manual map review. The model localizes and classifies spatial patterns, then projects masks to the die grid for die-level risk attribution. Adjacent risk is captured using the Good-Die–Bad-Neighborhood 3×3 matrix rule (GDBN9), expanding from defect "seed" dies to neighbors. Reliability is validated with burn-in hazard-curve analysis: wafers in the AI-segmented BAD cohort show 1.56× higher end-of-stress failure-in-time (FIT) than matched GOOD controls. The approach reduces manual intervention and enables consistent, reliability-aligned automation of disposition decisions.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551200 | Effect of Fluorine By-products of the Dry Cleaning on the Electrical Characteristics of Oxide Films
This study investigates the effect of the dry cleaning process, used as a pre-cleaning for gate oxide formation, on the electrical properties of the oxide film. We prepared non-pattern wafers treated with HF wet cleaning as reference condition and various dry cleaning conditions, constructing corona oxide semiconductor (COS) samples for measuring the electrical properties of oxide using film analysis and substrate testing (FAaST). According to FAaST analysis, dry cleaning induced a higher effective oxide charge (Qeff) in the oxide film than HF wet pre-cleaning. The observed negative shift in flat-band voltage (VFB) and the lowering of threshold voltage (VTH) allowed us to infer the formation of positive fixed charges within the oxide film. As we detected residual fluorine (F) even after post-annealing, we developed a model to simulate the chemical reaction between fluorine and the damaged silicon layer. This helps to explain the formation of charge within the oxide film. However, the charge formation was observed to decrease when post-annealing time was sufficiently increased. This phenomenon was proven through the relation equations describing the sublimation of byproducts and their diffusion into the Si lattice. Through our study on the post-annealing process of dry cleaning, we confirmed that it plays a decisive role in determining the integrity of the oxide layer.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551260 | Smart Solutions for CMP Scratch Control
Chemical Mechanical Polishing (CMP) is essential for global planarization in advanced semiconductor manufacturing, where its performance directly impacts device yield and reliability. Yet, scratch defects formed during CMP continue to limit yield and compromise device integrity, especially as device features shrink and integration schemes grow more complex. These scratches are typically caused by large particles dragged across the wafer surface particles that originate from interactions between the polishing pad, slurry, wafer, and conditioner. Common particle sources range from pad debris created during conditioning, to abrasive-slurry agglomerates, to residues from consumables and process byproducts, all of which are extremely sensitive to consumable choices and process settings.This work presents a comprehensive, system-level framework for CMP scratch mitigation across oxide, metal, nitride, polysilicon, and wafer-reclaim processes. We systematically address the full particle lifecycle from generation and transport to removal through coordinated optimization of consumables, process parameters, and tool hardware. Strategies include enhanced slurry filtration, optimized pad conditioning, implementation of a Less-Deposition-Less-Polish (LDLP) scheme, upstream process tuning, and hardware modifications such as high-pressure micro jet (HPMJ) cleaning.Experimental validation across multiple CMP modules using inline defect monitoring and macro inspection demonstrates substantial and sustained reduction in scratch-related defects. The proposed framework achieved significant improvement in scratch performance while maintaining required material removal rates for advanced technology nodes. These results establish a scalable and cost-effective methodology for CMP defect control, enabling improved process stability, yield enhancement, and device reliability in next-generation semiconductor manufacturing.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551208 | Gas Path Baffle Ring Tuning in Low Vacuum Chambers Utilizing Computational Fluid Dynamics
Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) analysis is increasingly becoming a critical tool in understanding complex fluid interactions internal to semiconductor process chambers. Gas processes such as Chemical Vapor Deposition (CVD), chemical etch, and photolithography, among others, require strict operating pressure and airflow controls; this, in turn, creates dynamic internal flow patterns that are not easily visualized. Wafers in vertical single wafer process chambers are directly situated beneath diffusive showerheads to aid in isotropic deposition/etch uniformity. Because of these isotropic designs it is common to have an off-center vacuum port which, without proper baffling, skews fluid paths and gas velocities which become dependent on vacuum pump distance magnitude. We show through CFD analysis a mathematical approach to tuning velocity spectra by refined adjustment of baffle ring channel diameters, leading to improved flow and physical deposition thickness uniformity.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551212 | Mutual Dopant Cross-Contamination in Wafer-FOUP Systems: Mechanisms and Suppression Strategies
This study investigates the mechanism and control strategies for a novel phenomenon in which boron dopants from the implantation process contaminate wafers held in wet-cleaned front-opening unified pods (FOUPs), potentially causing yield loss in semiconductor devices. A newly-developed plasma doping (PLAD) technique, employing high-dose, low-energy implantation, induces pronounced dopant cross-contamination between wafers and FOUPs. During PLAD, high-concentration boron species generated in the plasma react and migrate, forming particles and causing unintended dopant incorporation that leads to anomalous electrical behavior. To elucidate the underlying mechanisms, systematic secondary-ion mass spectrometry (SIMS) analysis of boron and fluorine distributions on wafer surfaces was performed under various process conditions. Our results indicate that conventional wet-cleaning protocols for FOUPs are insufficient to remove residual boron species. While barrier-material (BM) FOUPs provide partial mitigation, their effectiveness remains limited. In contrast, implementation of a load-port purge (LPP) yields the greatest reduction in boron contamination. These findings provide practical guidance for contamination control strategies in the rarely reported high-dose, low-energy implantation processes, and highlight the need for dedicated FOUP handling to ensure device reliability.

DOI 10.1109/ASMC69324.2026.11551250 | Process and Hardware Optimizations For Aluminum Interconnect Etch Defectivity Improvement
Aluminum (Al) etch is one of the critical backend-of-line (BEOL) metallization processes in dynamic random access memory (DRAM) manufacturing. Al etch profile and sidewall polymer passivation impact defectivity-related yield loss and backend quality and reliability. This paper presents a case study on 1β nm DRAM Al interconnect metal etch process, describing how reliability and yield can be improved by implementation of a new process with appropriate polymeric gas selection and optimized polymeric gas ratios, and adoption of new hardware that is designed to improve defectivity margin. This is important because as the dimension of devices scale down, the process margin between Al metal line over etch and under etch shrinks, with over etch causing irregular notches or pits (mouse bite defects) along the bottom of the metal lines resulting in backend reliability issues, while under etch (blocked etch) causes metal line shorts resulting in yield loss; balance between the over etch and under etch margin becomes critical. These process and hardware improvements have successfully eliminated mouse bite defects and reduced blocked etch defects by at least 80%.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00350 | Bond Wave Analysis of SiCN for Fine Pitch Hybrid Bonding
Hybrid bonding has emerged as a key technology in advanced three-dimensional (3D) integration, yet challenges for further bonding alignment accuracy remain towards below 200 nm pitch. The scaling error of the bonding alignment can be controlled by understanding of the bonding dynamics such as the bond wave. This study investigates the bonding dynamics with PECVD-SiCN as a superior dielectric material. The pre-bonding surface were characterized by VSFG spectroscopy and zeta potential analysis. Direct bonding experiments using 300 mm SiCN wafers demonstrate that bond wave propagation is governed by three critical factors: surface functional groups and carbon content. Interestingly, no correlation was found between bond wave speed and bond strength. These insights provide a deeper understanding of bonding mechanisms, facilitating process refinements for improved hybrid bonding quality.

This work proposes a novel ultra-thin wafer-level active device transfer process for 3D ICs, achieving sub-1 $\mu \mathrm{m}$ interlayer thickness with high alignment accuracy. To address heat dissipation challenges in densely stacked structures, alternative materials such as $\text{AlN}, \text{Al}_{2} \mathrm{O}_{3}$, and $\text{Si}_{3} \mathrm{N}_{4}$ are investigated. Bonding quality is evaluated through plasma activation and IR imaging, while COMSOL simulations were employed to assess thermal performance. AlN exhibited superior heat dissipation and bonding characteristics, supporting high-density integration and effective thermal management, advancing next-generation 3D IC architectures.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00092 | Enhancement of the Adhesion in Multilayer Redistribution Layers of Fan-Out Wafer Level Package for Memory Applications
Advanced packages with fan out wafer level package (FOWLP) technology connect chips with different technology nodes and utilize multi-layer redistribution layers (MLR) technology to connect an integrated circuit to external components. Generally, a redistribution layer (RDL) is composed of numbers of dielectric insulation (DI) layers and redistribution trace (RT) layers with copper. MLR must transmit electrical signals stably and it is required to be reliable in a harsh environment at the same time. In this paper, thermo-mechanical reliability of MLR structures in the next generation memory package was investigated and improved, focusing on stress manipulation and adhesion improvement; 1) stress was manipulated by MLR layer re-design to distribute the stress evenly and 2) an interfacial adhesion was enhanced by plasma treatment. By optimizing the MLR structures and having plasma treatment, the next generation memory package demonstrated superior reliability performance up to thermal cycle (TC)-B 1500 cycles showing without failures.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00296 | Time-Efficient Eye-Opening Estimation Method for High-Bandwidth Memory Interface Design with Equalizers
This paper proposes a time-efficient eye-opening estimation method for high-bandwidth memory (HBM) interface channels where the transmitter (TX) with equalizer circuits is involved. The layout-based model of the TX IO with equalizer circuits is replaced with the driver resistance and pad capacitance. The PWL function representing the driver characteristics is then determined from the output response of the TX IO, and is utilized to generate the input bit responses for the specific bit sequences used in this paper. The output response at each node is determined by mathematical equations including the reference impedance transformation to the driver and receiver impedances, collecting the 2-port S-parameters from n-port S-parameters, the transfer function calculations, and the summation of output responses for all input nodes. The output response at each node is analyzed to estimate the eye-opening. Since there are many computational tasks, the multiprocessing library in Python is used to solve them quickly. The eye-opening data of the proposed method from eight different channels are compared with SPICE simulations in terms of the evaluated r-square value from the scatter plot and the simulation times.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00136 | Inter-Die Gap-Filling with Varying Aspect Ratio Using PECVD Oxide for 3D Packaging: Model Prediction and Experimental Validation
For heterogeneous integration and 3D packaging, after arranging the chiplets or dies, inter-die gap needs to be filled with organic or inorganic gap-fill materials which provide mechanical support and enable further stacking. Thick Plasma-Enhanced Chemical Vapor Deposition (PECVD) oxide is being considered as a gap-fill material. One of the major challenges of this material is, the gap-fill ability is largely dependent on the aspect ratio of the gap which is determined by die-to-die spacing and die height. DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00015 | Optical and Electrical Characterization of a Compact Universal Photonic Engine
We present the inaugural wafer-level characterization results of the TSMC Compact Universal Photonic Engine (COUPE) based on a grating coupler (GC) geometry. The optical path within the COUPE stack is meticulously designed to minimize impacts on optical properties, such as insertion loss and wavelength shift. To achieve this, features including an embedded micro-lens array, a backside metal reflector, and anti-reflective coating (ARC) layers have been developed. Following process optimization, the optical insertion loss of a GC measured on COUPE consistently remains below 1.2 dB, matching that measured on the photonics integrated circuit (PIC) wafer. This indicates that there is essentially no additional loss from transitioning the system from PIC to COUPE. The 1 dB bandwidth is approximately 25 nm, which is about 5 nm larger than that at the PIC wafer level. The central wavelength across the 300 mm wafer is tightly controlled within ± 1.7 nm of the incident wavelength in the O-band region. Electrical and reliability results are also reported. Our findings demonstrate that COUPE holds significant potential for use in pluggable transceivers or copackaged optics (CPO) for advanced networking and artificial intelligence (AI) applications.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00251 | A Novel Fracture Mechanics Technique on Studying Passivation Crack Behavior for Advanced Si Package with Aluminum Redistribution Layer (AL-RDL) Routing
In the last several years, “Super-High-Density Metal-Insulator-Metal” (SHDMIM) technology development had represented a significant advancement in advcaned Si node electronic packaging, particularly in the realm of integrated circuits (ICs) and semiconductor devices. This innovative approach focuses on enhancing package performance by leveraging the unique properties of metal-insulator-metal (MIM) structures, which are known for their excellent electrical characteristics. Typically, SHDMIM structures are embedded between the Passivation-1 and Passivation-2 layers to achieve electrical isolation. However, it is important to note that passivation, being a brittle material, is susceptible to the cracking, particularly under the conditions of reliability temperature cycling tests. Such cracking may compromise the integrity of the passivation layers, potentially leading to SHDMIM failure. This study investigates the fracture analysis in the Al-RDL U-shaped passivation-2 structure using both simulation and experimental methods. Initially, the critical fracture energy $\left(G_{c}\right)$ for both of Pass2-1 and Pass2-2 that composing the passivation-2 layer is determined using the nanoindentation method. Subsequently, using developed fracture model incorporating both static and dynamic crack analysis to elucidate the fracture phenomena and provide insights into crack initiation and propagation. In the static crack modeling, the energy release rate (Err) is evaluated using the J-integral method. The simulation model validates against test data, thereby providing the critical crack length by establishing the relationship between $G_{c}$ and Err. Parametric studies on geometry and process conditions reveals that the energy release rate is enhanced by smaller AP spacing, thinner Pass2-1 layers, and higher processing temperature. In the dynamic crack modeling, the eXtended Finite Element Method (XFEM) with traction-separation laws was utilized, the crack initiation at the Pass2-1 corner, its penetration through the Pass2-1/Pass2-2 interface, and subsequent propagation to the Pass2-2 bottom edge are simulated, which aligns closely with experimental observations. The developed fracture mechanism technique has been validated and provided a design guideline to mitigate the crack driving force in Al-RDL passivation structures. This study provides critical insights and methodologies to enhance the reliability of SHDMIM capacitor devices by addressing crack-related failures in passivation structures.

The bond line thickness (BLT) of the TIM1 is consistently maintained within 50~60μm on the periphery and over 95% coverage without delamination across the entire fanout module. This is achieved through a refined dispensing pattern of the adhesive-type TIM1 around the liquid metal material's periphery, coupled with a dam structure near the fanout module's corners to ensure BLT as thin as possible by bonding with both the lid and the substrate. Crucially, the process characterization data not only closely aligns with the design targets established by thermal and mechanical co-simulation analyses but also offers competitive thermal performance with significantly reduced stress compared to solder TIM, as indicated by mechanical simulation results.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00146 | Ultra High Aspect Ratio Photo Resist for Cu Pillar Based Multi-Stack Fan-Out Package with Wide I/O LPDDR
Recently, there has been increasing interest in the execution of AI functions on mobile devices themselves, which requires a bandwidth of $200 ~\text{GB} / \mathrm{s}$ or more to perform this function, and the number of I/O pins rises sharply as the required bandwidth increases ($\sim 512$). In order to increase the number of I/O pins, Cu wire/pillar with a pitch of less than $60 \mu ~\mathrm{m}$ for chip multi-stack is required, but the process margin is insufficient with wire bonding used in conventional packages(minimum pitch: 60 um). Therefore, in this study, we aim to develop high AR(Aspect Ratio) PR(Photo Resist) that enables the implementation of Cu pillars for multi-stack of FOWLP. While the AR of Cu pillars applied to the currently commercialized AP chip is $1.7(\text{Cu}$ pillar CD $195 \mu ~\mathrm{m} {@}$ THK $340 \mu ~\mathrm{m}$), the required AR for wide I/O LPDDR is 8.1 or more(Cu pillar CD $27 \mu ~\mathrm{m} {@} \text{THK} \geq 220 \mu ~\mathrm{m}$), which has increased the development difficulty of PR materials for Cu pillars(High AR, maintaining vertical shape). The biggest challenge faced during this study was achieving high resolution CD, and positive and negative PR were compared and evaluated to achieve this. Although positive PR is advantageous in terms of CD resolution; however, it has significant risks of dissolution loss, PR collapse/crack, and leaching into the plating solution, while negative PR exhibits the opposite characteristics. The positive PR applied in this study is a CAR(Chemically Amplified Resist)-based system, which was mainly evaluated by splitting the composition of 1) main polymer structure and 2) PAG(Photo Acid Generator). The main polymer used is an acrylic polymer, which is relatively soft nature compared to PHS(PolyHydroxy Styrene), increasing its resistance to cracks. The carboxylic acid group formed by deprotection due to the acid from PAG has a higher solubility in developer than the hydroxyl group formed in PHS, which is advantageous for high resolution. Furthermore, introducing adamantane groups at the ends of the acrylic polymer improved the adhesion to adjacent polymers, thereby improving the resistance to development loss and plating solutions. Additionally, for via open control, the protection group of PAG were made bulky to reduce the acid diffusion length, thereby maintaining the uniformity of the top and bottom of the via. Moreover, by adding additive containing thiol groups, the formation of covalent bonds at the PR-Cu seed interface were induced to minimize PR collapse. Since negative PR is a system with the greatest disadvantage in resolution, enhancing transmittance and solubility in developers is the most important development goal. The main composition is acrylic polymer and PI(Photo Initiator), and transmittance is directly influenced by the structure of the PI, while solubility is affected by the acrylic polymer. To meet the above two items, by selecting highly transparent and reactive PI, the amount of PI was minimized so that light can penetrate to the bottom of the PR(2.6-fold increase compared to commercially available PR @ $240 \mu \text{mT}$) minimizing the footing of Cu pillars by increasing the curing degree at the bottom, and additionally strengthening shelf life(1 month@R.T). In addition, since low Mw&high acid value acrylic polymer was introduced, the solubility in the developer was maximized. As a result of the evaluation, positive PR showed Cu pillar diameter $21 \mu ~\mathrm{m} {@}$ THK $220 \mu ~\mathrm{m}$, AR 10.5, and negative PR showed Cu pillar $22 \mu ~\mathrm{m} {@}$ THK $220 \mu ~\mathrm{m}$, AR 10.0. According to the relationship between NA(Numerical Aperture) and DOF(Depth of Focus) applying a low NA ($\leq 0.10$) exposure tool is advantageous to form a vertical via shape in a high thick PR, but in our evaluation results, we successfully implemented a high AR Cu pillar by applying an exposure tool with NA 0.12~0.15. Through the evaluation of PR capable of achieving high AR, we have secured the potential to universally apply exposure tools with high NA and expect it to be expanded to the development of new advanced package line-ups.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00271 | An Effective 3D Thermal Network Integrated with Deep Learning for Improved Prediction of the 3D Thermal Properties of Complex Packaging Patterns
As patterns on semiconductor packages become more complex and extend into three dimensions, it becomes increasingly difficult to accurately determine the thermal properties of the package. Heat transfer simulation based on the original resolution of the pattern is limited in practicality due to the high computational cost. In this study, we developed a compact model based on effective thermal conductivity (ETC) that can be applied to 3D packages with complex pattern. The model consists of a thermal network using ETC, and it is designed to reflect more diverse thermal properties by introducing diagonal ETC in addition to the existing orthogonal ETC. The proposed compact model accurately predicts the thermal properties of the 3D package with an error within 1% compared to the original detailed model, and the temperature distribution is also predicted with high accuracy. In addition, the total time from simulation file generation to simulation and post-processing was reduced by about 90% compared to the detailed model, demonstrating excellent computational efficiency while maintaining high prediction accuracy. Furthermore, experimental validation using Peltier devices confirmed the validity of the simulation results. We have demonstrated that the in-plane and through-plane ETCs of the unit cells can be predicted with high accuracy by utilizing material distribution image-based data, 2D convolution neural network (CNN), and 3D CNN, which enables us to quickly obtain thermal property maps of new packages without additional simulations. The proposed model can be used to quickly analyze the thermal behavior of 3D packages with complex patterns, and is expected to provide high efficiency in terms of time and computational cost during the design and test stage.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00170 | Predictive Modelling of Thin Films Properties Using an Experimental and Simulation-Based Approach
Brittle thin films, such as silicon dioxide, silicon nitride, and silicon oxycarbide, are widely used in semiconductor devices, where their mechanical reliability is critical. However, characterizing the plasticity and fracture properties of these thin films at sub-micron scales presents significant challenges due to the limitations of conventional mechanical testing methods. Existing literature data often exhibit large variations, making reliable characterization difficult. To address these challenges, this study integrates finite element analysis (FEA) simulations with experimental nanoindentation data to establish accurate plasticity and fracture toughness (FT) of brittle thin film. The proposed approach employs FEA to replicate the nanoindentation process, generating load-displacement curves that are iteratively calibrated with experimental data to extract the elastic-plastic properties of thin films. This study combines nanoindentation with finite element analysis, simplifies and accelerates the prediction of fracture toughness for brittle thin films using a critical stress intensity factor, enhancing reliability testing in semiconductor devices. The results show strong agreement between simulated and experimental FT data, validating the methodology. It offers a more efficient and easier-to-implement alternative to traditional cohesive zone modeling, which typically requires multiple iterative adjustments.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00111 | A Study on the Improvement of Solder Joint Reliability of Module Products by IPL Soldering Method
In recent years, the demand for performance of modules and solid state devices (SSDs) in areas such as artificial intelligence (AI), electric vehicles, and data centers has increased rapidly, leading to an increase in heat generation from modules and SSDs. Due to the difference in thermal expansion coefficients between BGA packages and PCBs, repeated heating and cooling of the products cause cracks in the solder joints between them, which significantly affects the product's lifespan. Therefore, this study applied an intense pulsed light (IPL) soldering process to increase the product's lifespan. Unlike reflow, which utilizes conventional convection methods, IPL soldering uses white light to induce radiant heat and perform soldering with low power in a short time. In this study, we performed soldering of SAC305/SAC305 + LTS hybrid/full LTS solder joints using IPL soldering and confirmed that reliability was improved through thermal cycle tests. In addition, cross-sectional microstructure analysis was used to identify the mechanism of improved reliability.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00051 | EMIB-T (TSV) Advanced Packaging Technology EMIB's Next Evolution
Embedded Multi-die Interconnect Bridge (EMIB) packaging technology has been being widely adopted in Intel's FPGA, Server CPU, GPU, and data centric high-performance compute (HPC) segments for heterogeneous integration (HI) applications. Recently, emerging artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML) applications have spiked the demand for hyper large form factor (HLFF) data center GPUs and CPUs with significant on-package memory bandwidth (e. g. high bandwidth memory (HBM)) to meet exploding training, inference and learning workloads. As the next evolution of EMIB, EMIB-T (TSV) advanced packaging technology enables a direct power delivery path through EMIB bridges via through silicon via (TSV), from the package bottom to HBM dies. As a result, EMIB-T technology significantly improves package power delivery efficiency, enables ultra-high speed UCIe-A die-to-die (D2D) communications, and paves the way to reach massive bandwidth density capability. In addition, we provide the process flow comparison between EMIB and EMIB-T technologies and share the unique challenges that EMIB-T technology poses with respect to equipment, materials, and integrated process. Together, EMIB and EMIB-T (TSV) packaging technologies provide a cost effective, high yielding, and elegant design alternative to future AI systems beyond existing 2.5 D industry solutions, with the scalability to ultra large area die complexes and HLFF packages in the AI era.

As a result, the panel level RDL interposer package was successfully demonstrated as an alternative package platform for future HPC/AI products.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00240 | Challenges and Solutions in Measuring Copperpolymer Interfacial Adhesion Strength in Advanced Packaging
In advanced packaging products, fan-out structures provide superior performance but exist challenges due to the mismatch in the coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE) between copper and polymer layers, increasing the risk of delamination. In this study, we compare the adhesion strength of copper-polymer interfaces using the stud-pull test and the double cantilever beam (DCB) method. The results show that the studpull test exhibited intralaminar fractures within the material or fracture between the stud and the experimental samples, while the DCB method provides more efficient adhesion strength measurements. Therefore, we recommend using the DCB method to better evaluate copper-polymer interfacial adhesion strength before RDL designs and manufacture, reducing the time and cost of material selection and testing.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00028 | New Silicon Capacitor Solutions with Over 1mm - Thick Core Based Embedded Substrate for Extremely Large Package Platform
Due to the increasing demand for Artificial Intelligence (AI) technology and technological advancements, there is an industry trend of integrating multiple dies, including High Bandwidth Memory (HBM) and Dynamic Random Access Memory (DRAM), resulting in larger package sizes. As the package size increases, the substrate size becomes larger and thicker, making it difficult to use embedded capacitor solutions on the substrate. Typically, package designs that use Die Side Capacitor (DSC) are mounted on side by die. In this paper, the authors propose two embedding silicon capacitor solutions that can be used in extremely large packages with core thicknesses exceeding 1 mm to address these issues. [1] Firstly, the authors propose a silicon capacitor solution with dummy silicon attached. Due to the thickness of the silicon wafer used in Deep Trench Capacitor (DTC) is limited, it is not possible to manufacture the silicon capacitor with thickness exceeding 780 um. Therefore, the silicon capacitor is fabricated as a wafer, and then Die Attach Film (DAF) is attached to the rear side of the silicon capacitor wafer. To achieve the required thickness, the dummy wafer is then attached on top of the DAF. In contrast to conventional silicon capacitors, this method can achieve a thickness of more than 1 mm. Secondly, the authors propose Side Stacked Silicon Capacitor (SSSC) solution, in which multiple layers of silicon capacitor wafers are stacked and mounted laterally on core based substrate. The first layer begins with a dummy wafer, followed by subsequent layers of silicon capacitor wafer. The wafers are bonded using Non-Conductive Film (NCF), and copper bumps with a thickness of more than 50 um are used for electrical connection. To prevent cracks in silicon layers due to its thinness, a molding material is formed in the center of SSSC. The SSSC design offers the advantage of high capacitance and enables double sided connections, maximizing the effectiveness of package Power Delivery Network (PDN) connections. The silicon capacitor solutions proposed by the authors are applicable as embedded Capacitor (eCAP) solutions in extremely large package designs with core thicknesses exceeding 1 mm, such as those used in AI, High Performance Computing (HPC), automotive applications. These solutions contribute to improving System on Chip (SoC) performance by enhancing impedance characteristics and voltage stability. This paper presents a comparative simulation study of PDN performance when utilizing capacitor solutions as DSC and eCAP components in extremely large package designs. Power Integrity (PI)-based simulation results focus on impedance characteristics and voltage drop outcomes. In this experiment, an actual automotive SoC using a thick core substrate based package platform is evaluated.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00345 | A Novel Disaggregated Approach of Assembling Integrated Heat Spreader for Advanced Packages

A disaggregated approach of IHS assembly can be a great solution for complex semiconductor products. This approach helps to simplify complex IHS manufacturing process and enable advanced packages which otherwise would not be feasible due to the manufacturability issues or cost. The two critical components of assembling IHS using disaggregated method are assembly process flow and adhesive selection. This paper will focus on the impact of assembly flow and adhesives on the thermal interface material (TIM) performance and package shape behavior. It was found through data collection that the package coplanarity incoming to IHS attach can be reduced by 7 % if stiffener is attached before the die attach process as opposed to post die attach. It was also found that the package coplanarity can be reduced by 30 % when stiffeners are attached before flat plate IHS assembly.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00348 | Novel Packaging Platform Based on Bridge Dies with Top and Bottom I/O Connections on Standard Substrates
Recently, with the advent of the artificial intelligence (AI) era, chiplet systems have emerged as a new standard platform for advanced package designs. To achieve the same high speed and bandwidth as monolithic chips, chiplets are interconnected using either an “interposer” or a “bridge.” Despite the interposer's dominant role in current packaging technology, bridge dies are gaining increasing attention due to their flexibility and cost advantages. In this study, we propose a bridge-die-based packaging platform that eliminates the need for specific substrate processing. Traditional bridge die technology requires trench formation in the substrate for die embedding due to the thick silicon portion of bridge dies. To overcome this limitation, we developed a method for removing the silicon portion while retaining the thin organic redistribution layers (RDLs). This was achieved using conventional molding and grinding processes. The proposed approach also enables electrical connections to both upper-side chips and bottom-side substrates through microbumps and flipchip bumps, respectively. Metallographic analysis revealed a wellshaped and precisely aligned morphology with uniform wetting and intermetallic formation. The final 7 × 7 package consists of two system-on-chip (SoC) dies and eight high-bandwidth memory (HBM) dies interconnected by nine bridge dies. Basic reliability tests, including preconditioning, thermal cycling (TC), and unbiased highly accelerated stress testing (uHAST), demonstrated the package's robust mechanical integrity. The proposed platform is promising as it allows the continued use of existing packaging processes and materials while offering not only low cost and high yield but also excellent mechanical stability and enhanced flexibility in package design.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00089 | Carrier Warpage Improvement Using Nonphotosensitive Dielectric Material for High I/O Density Organic RDL Application in Future Advanced Packaging
In this paper, we have studied the feasibility of nonphotosensitive polyimide (non-PSPI) as inter-layer dielectric of organic redistribution layers (RDL). Warpage management has become essential requirement for yield manufacture improvement in the advanced packaging. As non-PSPI are not intrinsically limited by the mechanical and lithographic properties trade-off, low CTE and low cure temperature can be achieved, hence, effectively reducing warpage. We have simulated and compared using finite elements modeling (FEM) the warpage behavior of conventional PSPI, low curing temperature PSPI and non-PSPI candidates on different substrates conditions. We demonstrated non-PS dielectric can reduce the warpage by 79 % utilizing silicon substrate and 95 % utilizing ceramic substrate when compared to conventional PSPI in the RDL formation process. Crack appearance risk on stack via was also demonstrated to be reduced by 94 % when using nonPSPI as insulation material. In addition, we have performed via opening experimental testing of non-PS dielectric materials using excimer laser and dry etching techniques. Both methods showed comparable via critical dimension (CD) to conventional photo-defined materials. Even though process integration compatibility, insulation performance and reliability remain to be verified, non-PSPI can be considered as an alternative to conventional PID materials for organic RDL applications, particularly in terms of warpage control.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00337 | Novel Fault Isolation Methodology Applied on Nanoscale Defect in Fine Line RDL for Advanced Fan-Out Package
Over the past decade, the semiconductor industry has increasingly focused on packaging innovations to improve device performance and power efficiency, which have promoted the development of advanced packaging solutions for high-end applications. Fan-Out Chip on Substrate (FOCoS) packages with organic interposers and micro bumps have emerged as an effective packaging solution for heterogeneous integration. However, as defects become smaller in advanced packages, failure analysis (FA) has become more challenging, and identifying the root cause requires further investigation. To address this, ASE developed a novel fault isolation methodology to identify assembly issues in copper (Cu) redistribution layer (RDL) for failed FO packages with nanometer-scale defects, which could not be detected by standard FA techniques. In this case study, the novel fault isolation methodology proved to be effective for localizing defects within a failed package. This essential electrical FA technique isolates faults and identifies the defect mode in the failed package. The process begins with plapping technology to remove the stiffener, substrate, C4 bumps, micro bumps, and silicon dies, revealing the C4 bump pads and micro bump pads. Lock-in thermography is then used with the fault localization methodology to pinpoint the defect location through hotspot analysis. Next, a three-dimensional X-ray microscope (3D X-ray) is used to inspect the defect mode and verify whether the defect location aligns with the hotspot identified earlier. The defect mode of fine-line Cu RDL with 10 nm -scale defects was confirmed through Focused Ion Beam (FIB) observation. Elemental analysis was then conducted to identify the key factors contributing to the abnormal issue in the Cu RDL layer. With this novel fault isolation FA procedure, both the analysis methodology and results covering fault isolation and physical analyses are discussed in detail. The case study concludes with a summary of findings and the implications for further root cause investigation.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00213 | Simulation Analysis of Edge Dot-Void Formation and Edge Roll-Off Effects in Wafer-to-Wafer Bonding
Wafer-to-wafer bonding is a key technique in semiconductor manufacturing, enabling high-density 3D integration. However, defects such as edge dot-voids significantly impact yield and reliability. This study investigates the influence of edge roll-off on void formation using experimental analysis and multi-physics simulations. The results indicate that decreasing edge roll-off increases the occurrence of edge dot-voids and affects interfacial adhesion forces, which in turn modulate pressure variations leading to Joule-Thomson void formation. We propose a mechanistic explanation integrating van der Waals interactions and compressed air pressure effects. Simulation results align with experimental data, demonstrating that a nanoscale modification in wafer height can significantly influence macroscale bonding defects.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00336 | Microbump Interconnections Using Vertical Wire Technology for the Power Connections Between SoC and Substrate in the Bridge Die-Based Packaging Platform
In this study, vertical wire technology was employed to provide a power-line connection between a system-on-chip (SoC) and a substrate. Metallurgical bonding was performed between the vertical wires and the microbump of the SoC die after wire-ball bonding on the substrate. Palladiumcoated copper (PCC) wires and Au wires were used, and the bonding characteristics and electrical properties of the wires were examined. The soldering joint with the PCC wire was sufficiently robust to withstand the package structure, compared with the Au wire. By combining this technology, flexibility in the design of vertical interconnections can be achieved. The simulation of power integrity (PI) revealed satisfaction within the SoC specifications, even for the longest vertical wire in our condition. Electrical tests showed a much higher failure rate for the Au wire than for the PCC wire. This was due to the excessive Au-Sn intermetallics formed on the solder joint in the Au wire, whereas the PCC wire showed stable growth of the interfacial Sn-Cu intermetallics. This is the first study on a vertical wire applied to a power-connection design, and shows the potential in providing the 2.xD packaging solution from both performance and cost perspectives.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00267 | Leveraging Artificial Intelligence to Enable High-Fidelity Modeling of Substrate Thermo-Mechanical Behavior
Efficient, high-fidelity modeling of substrate thermomechanical behavior is required to address the demanding reliability challenges in today's advanced packaging technologies. The aim of this study was to develop a novel artificial intelligence (AI) method for efficient and accurate predictions of substrate thermomechanical behavior. An AI model was trained and tested with high-fidelity, ground-truth thermomechanical responses of a single substrate design, yielding a root-relative-squared error of $\leq 8.1 \%$. The warpage of two additional substrate designs was predicted at two temperature conditions (room-temperature and high-temperature) using: (1) an AI-enabled finite element model and (2) a design-agnostic reference model. The accuracy of warpage predictions was assessed against corresponding experimental warpage measurements. Qualitative and quantitative observations demonstrated that the AI-enabled model achieved accurate prediction of the complex shape, magnitude, and direction of the warpage (magnitude error $\leq 52.8 \%$, root-mean-squared error $\leq 28.6 \%$, and Mahalanobis distance $\leq 1.23$ standard deviations) and an overall improvement as compared to the design-agnostic model (magnitude error $\leq 117.8 \%$, root-mean-squared error $\leq 15.3 \%$, and Mahalanobis distance $\leq 1.89$ standard deviations). The AI enabled model achieved high-fidelity predictions in less than 5% of the time for predictions of equivalent fidelity and in less than 1% of the time-to-data for physical measurements. This method represents the best-known approach to substrate warpage and stress modeling and achieves sufficient predictive capability for advanced package design and process optimization, facilitating more rapid product development and enabling next-generation technologies.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00338 | Improving the Uniformity of Cu Deposition in Fanout Panel Level Package with FEM Simulation Model
In the packaging industry, Fan-out Panel Level Packaging (FOPLP) is recognized as an advanced technology. Compared to Fan-out Wafer Level Packaging (FOWLP), FOPLP offers significant advantages, including higher production capacity and reduced costs. Within the package structure, the copper redistribution layer (RDL) serves not only as an interconnection platform but also plays a key role in influencing the electrical and mechanical performance of the system. However, as the panel size and processing area increase, stable and uniform electrical and fluidic distributions need to be controlled during the electroplating process, otherwise the thickness of the RDL will be non-uniform. The uniformity of electroplating serves as a crucial metric for assessing plating performance, with the specification for manufacturing non-uniformity ($\text{NU} \%$) defined by a stringent threshold of less than 10 %. This study utilized the finite element method (FEM) in COMSOL Multiphysics ${ }^{(8)}$ to predict and analyze the thickness trends of plating copper, thereby saving both time and money. The kinetic parameters corresponding to the electroplating solution formulation used in simulation were obtained using a potentiostat. During the electroplating process, the contact pins on the holder make contact with the seedlayer of the cathode and apply a voltage to facilitate the reduction of metal ions, resulting in the deposition of a metal layer. The external potential is a critical factor that influences the electroplating process, as it can be adjusted to control both the thickness and uniformity of the plating copper layer. The study aimed to address the issue of plating non-uniformity in Fan-out Panel Level Packaging through simulation. This is the detailed discussion on how pattern firewall design, seedlayer, and contact pin influence plating uniformity. The findings of this research can be directly applied to solve manufacturing challenges.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00048 | Development of Embedded Multi Si Bridge Package in Panel Level Process for HPC/AI Applications
With the recent growth of the High Performance Computing and Artificial Intelligence markets, demand for advanced package which can integrate multi-chip is increasing. Fusion bonding with micron-thick dielectrics (e.g., $\text{SiO}_{2}, \text{SiN}$ and SiCN) is the incumbent technology for the permanent bonding of thermal/mechanical enhancement components in 3D heterogeneous integration. We demonstrate that the bonding energy matches or exceeds that of fusion-bonded oxide within a temperature range below $350^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$. Thermal analyses of different product architectures show that switching to this technology produces significant gains in cooling capability for high power and/or high-power density applications.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51687.2025.00209 | Thermal Modeling of IR Laser Debond with Inorganic Thin Film Release Layers
Infrared (IR) laser debond from silicon carrier wafers with inorganic thin film bond/debond layers enables new opportunities for 3D packaging and device fabrication by offering an assembly platform with sub-micron total thickness variation (TTV), the best thermal/mechanical stability, and compatibility with high temperatures and all silicon wafer tools and processes. An upper-bound temperature analysis is applied to show that there is no thermal risk to devices and no added thermal barrier layers are required. DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00170 | Physics-Informed Neural Network Approach for Fast Prediction of Temperature Distribution and Hot Spots in Co-Packaged Optics
Thermal gradients in co-packaged optics (CPO) increase thermal crosstalk in densely integrated multi-chip stacks and accelerate reliability wear-out. Fast prediction of three-dimensional (3D), multi-layer temperature distributions is therefore critical for early-stage package exploration and thermal-aware layout decisions. This paper presents a physics-informed deep learning framework that predicts volumetric temperature fields in heterogeneous CPO assemblies by embedding the steady-state variable-conductivity heat equation into the training objective via a finite-difference residual. A 3D U-Net maps voxelized thermal-conductivity and heat-source tensors to the 3D temperature distribution, while the physics loss enforces consistency with governing heat transfer without requiring extensive labeled datasets. Validation against ANSYS Icepak on both a photonic chip region and system-level multi-chip layouts demonstrates accurate hotspot prediction and inter-chip heat transfer modeling, achieving an average temperature-field prediction accuracy of 89%.

The diced substrates show no SeWaRe defect even after subjecting them to similar thermal shock conditions as the bare metallized cores tested above. As expected, we see a significant overlay improvement for Redistribution Layers (RDLs) built on glass core as compared to an organic core. We also report successful co-formation of both electrical TGVs and Optical waveguides in the same glass substrate. We measured a consistent low loss light transmission across the waveguides created in the glass. The results presented are significant advances paving the path for successful commercialization of this technology and enabling the scalability of the packages to finer design rules, ultra large area die complexes and Hyper Large Form Factor demanded by HPC and AI applications.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00362 | Reliability Assessment of Liquid Metal Alloy Thermal Interface Materials in Fan-Out Embedded Bridge (FO-EB) Packages
Liquid metal (LM)-based thermal interface materials (TIMs) are attractive for advanced packaging because of their low thermal resistance. This study evaluated the thermal performance and reliability of two LM-based TIMs, namely HS-TIM and HCF-TIM, employed in fan-out embedded bridge packages. The TIMs showed considerably lower interfacial thermal resistance than did conventional TIMs. Reliability evaluations under high-temperature and high-humidity conditions revealed that both TIMs experienced some performance degradation after unbiased highly accelerated stress tests (uHASTs). By contrast, HS-TIM showed pronounced degradation after long-term high-temperature storage because of thermo-oxidative hardening of its Si-based matrix. Overall, the results indicate that HCF-TIM is a promising next-generation TIM for advanced packaging.

As hybrid bonding interconnect (HBI) pitch continues to scale below 3 µm, post bond overlay requirement pushes current generation chip bonders to their accuracy limits. This bonder induced misalignment is only one of the challenges for pitch scaling, another challenge is the chiplet level distortion that occurs as the bond wave propagates during the chip-to-wafer (C2W) bonding process. While bonder accuracy keeps getting better year over year, chiplet distortion has become more prominent as one of the biggest contributors of the post bond overlay error. A systematic understanding of key contributors to chiplet distortion is essential for effective control and compensation throughout the C2W bonding process flow.The current study investigates chiplet distortion phenomena in C2W hybrid bonding (HB) process through both experimental and modeling-based approaches. Experimental data are collected to validate the FEA model, enabling accurate distortion predictions based on incoming chiplet conditions and bonding parameters.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00245 | An Innovative Automated Tracing Method for Defect Identification in Advanced FOCoS-Bridge Package
Fan-Out Chip-on-Substrate-Bridge (FOCoS-B) has emerged as an advanced next-generation packaging technology designed to enhance device performance and power efficiency in artificial intelligence (AI), high-performance computing (HPC), and networking applications. The FOCoS-B architecture leverages silicon bridge technology to enable chiplet integration, in which miniature silicon dies with multiple routing layers serve as in-package interconnects between Application-Specific Integrated Circuits (ASICs) and High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM). DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00025 | Low-Temperature Chip Scale Cu-Cu Hybrid Bonding by Electroless Ag Passivation
Cu-Cu hybrid bonding has demonstrated as a promising solution for three-dimensional integrated circuit construction. However, conventional Cu-Cu hybrid bonding processes face challenges such as the need for a high-vacuum environment, stringent surface condition requirements, and bonding temperatures exceeding 300 °C, which can induce chip warpage and device degradation. To address these issues, this study proposes a low-temperature, ambient die-level Cu-Cu hybrid bonding process. An Ag passivation layer was deposited on the Cu pads of a 30-micrometer-pitch test vehicle via an electroless plating process. The electrical test yield of Cu-Cu hybrid bonding with an Ag passivation layer showed significant improvement compared with the conventional Cu-Cu bonding baseline processed at temperature below 250 °C. Overall, this study demonstrates that electroless Ag passivation enables Cu-Cu hybrid bonding under low-temperature, ambient environments with less stringent surface requirements, offering strong potential for future three-dimensional integrated circuit packaging applications.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00161 | Effects of Plating Current Density on Electrochemical Reliability of RDL: In-Situ Electrochemical Migration Testing and Time-to-Failure Prediction
As semiconductor packages continue to advance toward higher integration densities, the reliability of the redistribution layer (RDL) has become increasingly critical. Scaling-driven reductions in dielectric spacing and linewidths can heighten susceptibility to dielectric failure modes, including time-dependent dielectric breakdown (TDDB). Among electrical reliability concerns, electrochemical migration (ECM) is widely recognized as a dominant failure mechanism, in which anodic copper dissolution and cathodic dendrite growth can lead to short-circuit failures. The kinetics of ECM are strongly dependent on copper grain size, which is in turn governed by the applied plating current density (ASD, A/dm2). In this study, the electrochemical properties of copper varying with ASD were characterized using linear polarization resistance (LPR) and potentiodynamic polarization (PDP). Furthermore, the moisture diffusivity of the photo-imageable dielectric (PID) under Biased Highly Accelerated Stress Test (B-HAST) conditions was evaluated via electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS). The microstructural variations of copper induced by the applied ASD, particularly grain size, were analyzed using X-ray diffraction (XRD). B-HAST was conducted to validate the effect of microstructure characteristics-dependent electrochemical kinetics on reliability performance. Failure analysis using Focused Ion Beam (FIB) cross-sectioning was performed to verify the failure mechanism and dendrite morphology. Finally, incorporating these experimentally derived material parameters, a finite-element simulation model was developed to predict failure time, successfully establishing a comprehensive predictive model linking manufacturing process parameters to electrochemical reliability.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00289 | Design and Optimization of 2-mm Staggered RDL Interposer Channels for UCIe 3.0 up to 64 GT/s

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00398 | Robust Design of Hybrid Bonding Considering Cu Pad Inelastic Deformation for Reliability
The rapid advancement of artificial intelligence (AI) is driving increasing demand for high-bandwidth memory (HBM). Replacing conventional micro-bump interconnects with bumpless hybrid bonding can improve energy efficiency and thermal performance, enabling higher-performance computing. However, manufacturing-induced variations such as misalignment and dishing pose critical reliability challenges. In this study, the thermo-mechanical effects of these process variables on bonding integrity are analyzed using three-dimensional (3D) finite element method (FEM) simulations incorporating mechanical inelasticity. Normal stress, shear stress, and stress triaxiality are employed to characterize compressive bonding strength, shear instability, and void nucleation potential. Systematic variation of pad diameter and dishing depth reveals that bonding-related compressive stress can vary by more than a factor of three, underscoring the strong sensitivity to process-dependent design parameters. Increasing the diameter-to-height ratio D/h improves the interfacial stress state. The thin pad design achieves approximately 1.5 times higher compressive stress than the reference design, maintaining bonding stability even with up to 20% misalignment. The bonding interface failure is shown to originate from triaxiality fluctuations caused by misalignment-induced shear instability. Edge cracking worsens with misalignment, as the Cu–dielectric coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE) mismatch triggers shear stress while preventing sufficient normal bonding pressure. Based on these findings, a robust guideline is to increase D/h and minimize dishing depth within process limits to suppress both center voids and edge cracks.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00301 | IR-Drop Analysis of Power Delivery Networks in 3D Stacked Dies Using Face-to-Face and Face-to-Back Integration

This paper presents an integrated steady-state IR-drop analysis workflow for TSV-based 3DIC that links early design-space exploration, commercial RTL-to-GDS implementation, and sign-off multi-die co-simulation in a single loop. An IR-drop contribution breakdown links these trends to shared current flow through TSV/backside delivery segments in F2F, while F2B largely confines TSV current to top-tier delivery. Finally, we implement a reduced-order early IR-drop estimator and validate it against sign-off results with a worst-case error of 10.6 percent.

The present work introduces a comprehensive multiscale framework to characterize the thermo-mechanical evolution of liquid epoxy molding compound (EMC) under industrial compression molding conditions. Central to this approach is the rigorous characterization of the EMC’s transition from a viscous, zero-modulus state to a load-bearing viscoelastic solid. Sensitivity analyses definitively show that superior warpage modeling requires simultaneous consideration of all cure-dependent thermo-mechanical properties. The integration of atomistic-to-macroscopic properties establishes a new standard for high-accuracy warpage prediction, essential for ensuring the structural integrity of next-generation semiconductor packages.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00162 | Deep Coordination Graph-Based Reinforcement Learning for Multi-Domain PDN Optimization
This paper presents a hierarchical multi-agent reinforcement learning framework based on Deep Coordination Graphs (DCG) for simultaneous multi-domain Power Distribution Network (PDN) optimization in heterogeneous AI accelerator PCB designs. Traditional sequential optimization approaches suffer from greedy failures, where early-optimized domains monopolize limited PCB resources, rendering solutions for subsequent domains infeasible. To overcome this, the proposed framework first generates routing skeletons and Voronoi-based spatial partitions to initialize the environment, then employs DCG-based MARL to enable all power domains to optimize simultaneously through explicit coordination. By decomposing the global value function into individual and pairwise terms, agents learn to achieve their own DC resistance (DCR) targets while minimizing interference with neighboring domains. Physics-informed feedback via real-time modified nodal analysis guides agents toward voltage drop hotspots, significantly reducing the search space. Validated across 23 unseen test configurations, the proposed method achieves a success rate of 82.6%, outperforming Random Search (76.4% on average) and GA with 1000 number of function evaluations (NFE) (GA-1000, 59.4%), while achieving over 1,000 times speedup compared to GA. These results demonstrate the practical viability of the proposed framework as an efficient solution for real-time PDN design automation in next-generation high-density systems.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00117 | High Power System Scaling Using Double-Sided Connection in Embedded Substrate

The heat trapped by the embedded die can shorten its lifespan, degrade performance, and compromise product reliability. To fully leverage the benefits of die embedding, it's essential to first solve the thermal issue while enabling efficient electrical performance.This paper introduces a new manufacturing process: the double-sided connection technique, designed to overcome these thermal and electrical challenges. Through dedicated process development and continuous improvement, we have successfully developed the capability to embed not just a single die, but also three or more dies simultaneously. This work presents a scalable and robust platform for high-performance computing, providing a clear path to enhanced product reliability and performance for the most demanding applications.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00131 | System-Level Thermal Characterization of Hybrid Cu Bonded HBM on 2.5D Advanced Packaging
Hybrid Copper Bonding (HCB) is emerging as a key next-generation chip-to-chip bonding technology in high-performance computing (HPC) systems due to its ultra-fine pitch capability and reduced interconnect resistance. As device power density continues to increase, bonding technology selection critically influences not only electrical performance but also system-level thermal behavior and reliability. While thermo-compression bonding (TCB) has been widely adopted, the thermal advantages of HCB relative to TCB have not been quantitatively established, particularly under realistic 2.5D integrated operating environments. In this work, a physics-based multi-scale numerical modeling methodology is developed to capture the thermal characteristics of TCB and HCB interconnect structures from the chip-scale to the system-level. The framework resolves detailed chip-scale interconnect geometries, including metal connectivity and dielectric thin-layer effects, and propagates their thermal characteristics to package- and server-level simulations through effective thermal property extraction. Experimental measurements using HCB and TCB HBM test vehicles integrated with ASIC test vehicles on silicon interposers are performed under controlled server-level air-cooling conditions to validate the modeling approach. The results demonstrate that the proposed methodology accurately reproduces measured thermal behavior and reveals the intrinsic thermal advantages of HCB over TCB, including reduced hot-spot junction temperature, mitigated thermal crosstalk and increased allowable power. Parametric studies on HCB joint pad density identify design configurations that further enhance heat dissipation efficiency. The developed methodology provides a predictive design framework for evaluating bonding technologies and guiding thermal optimization in next-generation HPC packaging architectures.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00094 | System-Level Thermal Validation of 2.5D Packages in GPU Servers: Impact of TCB vs HCB HBM Platforms
The scalability and long-term reliability of 2.5D System-in-Package (SiP) platforms are increasingly governed by complex thermal management requirements, particularly as the integration of High-Bandwidth Memory (HBM) introduces concentrated heat profiles that challenge the system’s operational limits. The package platform—Thermo-Compression Bonding (TCB) versus Hybrid Copper Bonding (HCB) of HBM—strongly influences intra- and inter-package thermal behavior. This work implements 2.5D system-in-package (SiP) thermal test vehicles (TTVs) in an Open Compute Project (OCP)-standard GPU server with embedded sensors and controllable heaters across HBM stacks and GPU dies, faithfully mirroring functional heterogeneous package floorplans. Experimental results demonstrate thermal nonlinearity - strong platform- and cooling-dependent. At 1030 W per package, HCB reduces intra-package GPU to HBM thermal crosstalk versus TCB by 2.2% under air cooling and 9.8% under liquid cooling, while inter-package thermal crosstalk varies by up to 13.7% across cooling conditions. Comparative evaluation confirms that HCB measurably improves thermal conduction, reducing both intra- and inter-package thermal resistance. From a data-center perspective, the reduction in GPU to HBM crosstalk resistance enables up to 0.9°C higher allowable coolant inlet temperature in liquid cooling relative to the TCB baseline, which translates to approximately 3% cooling power reduction and PUE improvement from 1.26 to 1.24. For a 1000-rack AI cluster, this corresponds to roughly 31 GWh annual energy savings. Measured thermal trends further indicate that as AI infrastructure evolves toward inference-heavy, memory-focused workloads with increased HBM base-die power, HCB platforms will deliver progressively larger thermal benefits due to the shift toward more vertical-resistance-limited behavior. This study establishes GPU server-integrated 2.5D SiP TTV methodology as a robust platform for system-level thermal validation and demonstrates that HBM platform selection directly impacts data-center operational efficiency and future inference scalability.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00326 | Novel Microbump Structure of Direct Solder Bumping on Seed Layer with Barrier Functionality (SoSBF)
As bump pitch continues to shrink in next-generation high-bandwidth-memory (HBM) stacks, the allowable solder volume and the thickness of the under-bump metallization (UBM) must be reduced in order to keep the joint gap within a few micrometers. Conventional Ni-based UBMs, while serving as diffusion barriers, readily react with SnAg solder to form low-conductivity Ni3Sn4 intermetallic compounds (IMC). When the solder layer is thinned, IMC formation occurs earlier, consuming the limited solder; at the same time, thinning the Ni-UBM degrades its barrier function, allowing further metal inter-diffusion and continuous IMC growth. Under prolonged thermal stress, the expanding IMC layer induces volumetric shrinkage, which leads to joint failure and a loss of long-term reliability. To overcome these coupled challenges, we introduce a direct Solder-on-Seed-layer with Barrier Functionality (SoSBF) structure. The SBF is a 0.2 μm thickness that is thermodynamically stable against Sn, thereby suppressing IMC formation, preventing unnecessary solder consumption, and preserving interfacial stability during both the bonding process and extended thermal-stress exposure. This SoSBF structure enables robust, IMC-free bonding while shrinking the joint gap to 1.0 μm and significantly reducing thermal resistance, making it a promising solution for ultrafine-pitch HBM stacking.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00133 | Fine-pitch Cu-Cu Hybrid Bonding Using Electroless-deposited (111) Nanotwinned Cu
To enable the high-density interconnections required for advanced packaging in Artificial Intelligence (AI) and High Performance Computing (HPC) applications, hybrid bonding has emerged as a key enabling technology. Compared with conventional fabrication methods, electroless deposition provides a straightforward and autocatalytic approach for the synthesis of nanotwinned copper (nt-Cu). This work demonstrates the uniform and selective electroless deposition of highly (111)-textured nt-Cu pads on a fine-pitch polyimide (PI) hybrid bonding test vehicle (10 μm diameter, 30 μm pitch). Both theoretical calculations and experimental results indicate that a Cu surface protrusion is vital for achieving high-quality hybrid bonding due to the higher coefficient of thermal expansion (CTE) of PI relative to nt-Cu. Accordingly, an optimized Chemical Mechanical Planarization (CMP) process was developed to establish a surface topography featuring protruding Cu pads and a recessed PI region, as verified by atomic force microscopy (AFM). This enabled the formation of robust nt-Cu/PI hybrid joints bonded at 250 °C and 10 MPa for 1 hour, without the need for post-bonding annealing, as revealed by focused ion beam (FIB) cross-sectional analysis. These results highlight the potential of electroless deposited nt-Cu for the next-generation fine-pitch packaging.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00262 | In-Situ Observation and Modeling of Crack Velocity at Wafer-to-Wafer Bonding Interface
Wafer-to-Wafer W2W) bonding directly joins two wafers and is vital for achieving high integration density in 3D integrated circuits. Conventional reliability evaluation, such as the Maszara test, measures bonding energy by inserting a blade to induce a crack and recording its final propagation length. This approach provides valuable insight into the reliability of W2W bonding, but it has limitations in the amount of information that can be obtained for analysis. This paper introduces an in-situ observation method to monitor crack propagation during the Maszara test using a vision system. Real-time image processing enables measurement of crack velocity, offering deeper insights into bonding reliability. Experiments were conducted on two dielectric materials (DM1 and DM2) with similar bonding energies. Despite this similarity, DM2 showed up to 10× faster crack propagation than DM1, revealing that crack dynamics depend on more than bonding energy alone. Two models were developed to interpret this behavior. The analytical model incorporates crack velocity as a function of energy release rate, predicting both crack speed and final length consistent with experiments. The cohesive zone model, implemented into the finite element method, confirmed that variations in stiffness—rather than maximum stress—govern crack propagation speed. Overall, combining in-situ testing and modeling demonstrates that materials with identical bonding energies can exhibit markedly different crack behaviors, enabling more comprehensive evaluation of W2W bonding reliability.

DOI 10.1109/ECTC51846.2026.00149 | IR Laser Debond Process Modeling
Infrared (IR) laser debond from silicon carrier wafers with inorganic thin film bond/debond layers enables new opportunities for 3D packaging and device fabrication by offering an assembly platform with sub-micron total thickness variation (TTV), the best thermal/mechanical stability, and compatibility with high temperatures and all silicon wafer tools and processes. Models of the IR debond process are needed to guide process development, integration, and design for specific applications. In this paper, we present a model of the IR laser debond process, which can be applied broadly to evaluate feasibility and guide optimization of process parameters and thin film materials and thickness. The model is applied to design a dual-function carrier wafer backside film for both wafer bow correction and anti-reflection to enhance the IR debond process. Experimental validation demonstrates strong agreement with model predictions, demonstrating 48% reduced wafer bow while increasing laser release layer (LRL) absorption to enable matched debond spot size with 20% lower laser power. IR laser shield layer effects were also systematically investigated, with the model predicting transmittance and LRL absorbance as a function of shield layer thickness and material. Finally, predicted temperature profiles in the LRL during the laser pulse are studied as a function of thickness, material properties, and pulsewidth. Model predictions are compared to experimental data on laser ablation threshold as a function of LRL thickness, and show qualitative agreement except for a small range as the thickness approaches zero. The modeling approach provides a framework for predicting and optimizing IR laser debond processes across diverse semiconductor packaging applications.
